I would use the remaining time to argue under this law to point to one dimension that has been somewhat neglected in my court, and since it is only a first reading that I believe that there is enough time left for the promoter to consider it together with all of us. I believe that in the spirit of general commitment to strengthening parliamentaryism in the Republic of Croatia, the law should make an additional step in the process of democratisation and strengthen the role of the Croatian Parliament in the area of defence. I think my earlier involvement in this system gives me the right to point him out here in particular. At first, I would stress that this topic is not specific only for Croatia, in a similar way a whole series of political and academic discussions are being held in Europe and the world. It is generally recognised that there is a particular lack of transparency in the defence sector as a consequence of the need to provide vital information for national security. It is also evident that in the defence area, executive power plays an important role in which parliament is rarely bypassed. It should also be pointed out that in the area of transparency in the defence sector, movements have been made that have also been recorded in the investigation into the defence corruption index published by Transparency International yesterday, and it found that 70% of the government has failed in an adequate way to deal with the problem of corruption. Croatia belongs to the other 30%, although it should add that there is a whole range of findings and recommendations that can be improved even further. For these reasons, I believe that in this debate it is necessary to point out the existence of a political will to strengthen not only parliamentary supervision, you call it civil, mainly in the west is a term used by parliament oversight, but also stronger and more inclusive in the defence area itself. In fact, the law in Article 5 recognises the role of the Parliament, this is what the Deputy Minister spoke about, but it seems insufficiently highlighted to me in comparison with other parliaments of a similar democratic system that we want to develop in Croatia. First of all, I am referring to the fact that Parliament's role in the affairs of larger procurement, i.e. the equipment of the Armed Forces, is not foreseen by means of combat over a certain amount. I'd like to give you a brief explanation. Equipment of material and technical means, particularly in the context of modernisation, is one of the most complex jobs in the jurisdiction of the Ministry of Defence. The countries that are our partners in NATO in the European Union have approached these problems differently, but always with the purpose of making this demanding job as transparent as possible. Unfortunately, in the past, Croatia has experienced certain negativity in this area, some of which are still waiting for a court epilogue. A response to similar problems in the West has been found not only in strengthening parliamentary surveillance, but also in including parliament, and thus the highest public in the process of major military procurement. For example, in our case, the solution to the issue of combat aviation, new ships, the development of certain types of weapons and equipment, is a matter of great importance to the state. At the same time, decisions which are not simple and are not uniform must include specific knowledge from several areas: military, strategic, financial, negotiating. In the end, these decisions are always political, they are long-term, and I therefore believe that the Parliament, its Defence Committee, is a particularly important place of debate, and a certain guarantee of implementation, especially in the event that the logic of democratic processes also leads to changes in power. It is also a very complex and complex shopping process in which the state still appears as the main participant of the business. In its 2006 survey, the IMF estimated that around 15% of the total consumption of weapons worldwide goes to various types of bribery and bribery. As a result, many democratic states have legally regulated a solution requiring formal consent of parliaments that de facto approve contracts in the defence area that cross a certain threshold, i.e. value, e. One million kunas or one million euros. In addition, specifically for countries that do not have extra highly specialised personnel to decide on such matters, which is our case too, is that they are currently using Parliament or a specialised committee to respond more successfully to this challenge. The involvement of Parliament means the involvement of more people with political responsibility, but also the public and civil society in a business that is of vital interest to the defence of the country. I think the solution I advocate would ease the burden of making decisions like this, which has been mostly at the Ministry of Defence in the past, and then in the process of making decisions in the government and the president. As I have already said, there has always been a tendency in developed societies to consider the defence to be a power shared by the military and the executive power. This was justified by the fact that decisions must be made quickly and efficiently. Those times are behind us for at least two reasons. One is the development of democratic procedures that inevitably and in this area mean greater involvement, and I would say Parliament's responsibility. The second, of course, is that we are not threatened by any direct threat. In this respect, the Parliament should also play a more important role in the process of evaluating the strategic defence review. Namely, reports on the process of reviewing the coherence of strategic concepts, as well as the achieved and planned defence capabilities with defence needs arising from the reality of the strategic and security environment as defined by this law are actually an ideal document for discussion in the Assembly and in the Assembly Committee. Because the fundamental purpose is, now we're talking about the strategic defence review theory just to strengthen the discussion and thus the decisions on vital interests, the ways in which they are protected and the resources to be created. We also have a body that was created as a consequence of our constitutional solutions. At the same time, he came to us in the parliamentary procedure and the Law on Service in the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia, so I believe that we can freely call this legislative step a small defence reform. Why do we need this reform and it's important? First of all, the current Defence Act was passed in 2002. Since then, it has only been changed several times, in 2004, 2007 and 2009. However, these changes were not more significant than those of 2007 which we needed to meet the conditions for establishing international cooperation and interoperability in the NATO collective defence system, whose membership we had already met. Since nearly six years have passed since the adoption of the original text of the law, it is logical that the Ministry of Defence, together with all relevant institutions whose powers in the defence portfolio have come under the law, have joined the creation of a new law. On one occasion, the Minister of Defence recently told us very honestly that the Law on Defence along with the Law on Armed Forces, or on the Service in the Armed Forces, is a small passage and that in practice there are problems in the functioning of the Armed Forces. Fortunately, the criticism did not stop, so we have before us the proposals of both new laws, key to the functioning of the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia. The fundamental law of defense resists a series of news. We consider it well-prepared and ultimately goes into two readings and is prepared as a whole new law instead of as changes and additions and as a club of SDP we support and welcome. It is in itself a co-ordination that with the new position of the Armed Forces within NATO and the EU in the future and no less important alignment with the new Constitution of the Republic of Croatia. First of all, the newspapers we want to welcome concern the transparent arrangement of institutions in the defence portfolio from the president of the Republic of Croatia, the Prime Minister, the Minister of Defence, the Chief of Staff of the Armed Forces, the Defence Council, the Council, the Council of Defense, the Council, etc. Since this is only the first reading and we will have the opportunity to discuss this law once again, I am taking the opportunity to highlight from this place a good thought that has been permeated through previous discussions on this law, which concerns the composition of the Defence Council. So, he was the chairman of the Defence Committee or of another member of the Defence Committee, and perhaps the best solution would be that he was elected from the ranks of Croatian Parliament MPs by the majority of the votes of all MPs. It's just a suggestion to think about. This solution, we believe, would be a significant step in parliamentary surveillance of the defence portfolio in the Republic of Croatia, and the strengthening of parliamentary supervision within NATO and between its members has long been referred to as a very important topic, and in this sense, the NATO Member States have different experiences and solutions. However, all tend to one goal, which is to strengthen parliamentary defence and security control mechanisms. Furthermore, I am also taking the opportunity to mention the provisions that speak of co-operation, training, action of members and troops of the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia. They are a new law in detail and it is evident that there is a great emphasis on interoperability. The previous law regulated this portfolio at a time when the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia were somewhat isolated, were not part of NATO or the EU at all, and the situation in the region was rather politically and securely unstable. The new Law on Defence takes place in time, but also with the Strategic Plan of the Ministry of Defence of the Republic of Croatia in 2013-2015, which has objectives divided into three groups. Therefore, the overall goal of the number one is to develop and maintain defence capabilities, the general objective of two meaningful and sustainable contributions to collective defence and international security and the general objective of three aid to the country's civilian institutions. New times bring new challenges to defence, and I believe the new Defence Act will allow the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia to respond successfully to them. The SDP club will support the adoption of the Defence Act, and I will now leave the second half of my time to my colleague Spoljar. Thank you.
ParlaMint-HR_2013-01-30-0.u28498	Vice-President of the Croatian Parliament, distinguished deputy ministers and colleagues and fellow MPs. What is a military call and service in the Armed Forces, and what about military invitation and service does this law propose to amend, supplement and improve? The armed forces are specially formed an organization trained and equipped to lead all forms of armed combat. The main task of the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia is to protect its sovereignty and territorial integrity. Armed forces may also carry out certain tasks in peace caused by exceptional circumstances, e.g. It's an elemental accident. Armed forces are responsible for implementing decisions by the competent civil authorities on the issue of war or on the issue of peace. At the service of the Armed Forces other than the active military personnel, the handbook, cadets and reserves are both civil servants and military deputies. The service in the Armed Forces is the service of military service, voluntary military training, cadet service, military service, reserve service and civil servant service. This law prescribes, for example, who can be a handbook, and the contractual reserve is also called and referred to the service of the Armed Forces in accordance with the provisions of the Defence Act and the provisions of this law. An adult citizen of the Republic of Croatia who fulfils the general conditions laid down by this law under model 3+5+5 may be admitted to the Armed Forces Service. The candidate signs a 3-year military service contract with the Ministry of Defence. In accordance with the needs of the service with the active soldier, a second military service contract shall be concluded for up to 5 years, after which it may be selected and with personal consent addressed to the first level of honorary education. And according to needs and third, for another five years. The proposed law also introduces a brand-new category of military specialists, already spoken of by the deputy. The life and work of members of the Armed Forces compared to civilian activities has been organised and formed. The requirements for respecting and respecting the rules of military discipline are strict, and his disrespect is punished. Military regulations determine the manner of official communication between soldiers and commanders, officers and officers. It is an obligation for every member of the army to wear a military uniform with signs of rank and position in the workplace. Until now, the above is governed by the Law of Service in the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia. It regulates access to the service of the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia, the schedule, acts and promotions to acts, obligations, rights and responsibilities, the separation and termination of the service and other status issues of members of the Armed Forces. But he is ten years old and it is necessary to comply with the Constitution, with the law, international war and humanitarian law and general and special rules of conduct. So today we have before us this proposal of the Law of Service in the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia. The proposed law prescribes principles and specialities in the service of the Armed Forces. For example, with a number of restrictions and obligations of a military person, officials and deputies have certain rights and privileges. Military employees' salaries on average are somewhat more than wages in similar civil occupations. This law regulates salaries, salary allowances and other material rights of the active military person. The salaries of active military personnel consist of basic salaries and salary supplements, pay additions are made by the active military person for special conditions of military service and for deficiting calls and professions. Military units, administrations and institutions have a different start and end of working hours, but they all work eight hours a day. In certain situations, a person in the armed forces is obliged to work overtime. This is exactly where the problem arose in paying overtime and paying compensation by lawsuits, and this proposal will change. The law proposes to arrange working hours, holidays and holidays. Some of the restrictions described by this law are to be imposed on an active military person, a civil servant and an undersigned shall not be permitted to carry out independent economic or professional activities. Military personnel do not have the right to trade union organisation and are not allowed to strike, and are not allowed to join political parties. Many jobs and tasks in the Armed Forces are carried out by women, their position in the Armed Forces should contribute to the establishment of a Committee on Gender Equality. Professional development of active military personnel is based on continuous training, training and education through successful conduct of duty, and promotion to higher rank, which also describes this law. The Law on Service in the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia also regulates the area of very, very important area of participation of members of the Armed Forces in peacekeeping operations and other activities abroad, by prescribing the rights and obligations of persons referred to in operations, support for peace and other activities abroad for a maximum period of 4 years. It must be at least 2 years between two references. Finally, this law stipulates that an active military service may cease under the law on the basis of a personal request after the expiry of the period on which it was admitted to the service or termination, following the expiry of the probationary work of soldiers and of the agreement. Active military personnel receive an honorable or dishonest discharge through the termination of their military service. It is prescribed in which cases an unfair discharge is received and the consequences of such dismissal are produced, and how it contributes to the discipline and reputation of the Armed Forces. The proposal for this law has no basis in politics, but is supporting reforms and the development of the Armed Forces of the Republic of Croatia. He proposes regulating education and employment in the Armed Forces, but at the same time strengthens the discipline and availability of the forces. Two readings open up extra space for the dialogue. The SDP's representative club supports this proposal. Thank you.

In particular, because Article 45(1) states that the Croatian Parliament, President of the Republic, Prime Minister and Defence Minister and in accordance with the Constitution and this law decide in the application of the Armed Forces. As regards Article 9 for the rescue service that Mr. Milas spoke of, in fact, unlike the existing law in this proposal within the Defence Council, there is no director of the State Administration for Protection and Rescue. I believe that, at the very least, it is necessary to explain why it is not proposed, given the importance of this institution in the area of national security. In Article 13, it is likely that a technical error: "The Ministry of Defence shall carry out matters relating to, among other things, the 19th military maritime affairs and military air traffic." And that's the same thing in Article 15. "The head office within the Ministry of Defence is performing tasks related to military-maritime operations and military-air transport operations." Article 13 and Article 15 define the same tasks that point to certain parallelism in the system, I assume that these transactions are carried out both at Ministry of Defence and at the level of the main headquarters. But everyone with certain powers, so I suggest that this difference be indicated in the text of the law. In Article 19, which Mr Novak is about, it seems to me: "The women who have special knowledge and skills needed by the armed forces are subject to the obligation to serve in the reserve." There is no indication of what special knowledge and skills it is, perhaps they are somewhere and if they are, then the act should be specified. Article 63, it's important that I come from the local self-government unit, and I know that there have been some problems related to this issue. Armed forces can provide exceptional assistance to local communities, civil society institutions in the implementation of humanitarian projects and projects of general interest. When carrying out the activities of paragraph 1 of this Article, the principles of free competition shall not be violated. Unlike the applicable law which does not regulate the issue of helping the local community and civil society institutions implement humanitarian projects and projects of general interest, this proposal allows for the proper regulation of this highly sensitive area. Of course, the most important thing is to make sure that the principles of free competition are not violated. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. It may be useful to draw up a list of all the implementing rules because one part of them cannot be properly read from this bill. And in conclusion, not to be too long, it is acceptable to regulate the area of duty and the rights of citizens to defend and use the Armed Forces. In some parts, it may be general, communication and information affairs, and in some it is pretentious, military-police affairs. And although the implementation of this bill does not require additional financial resources to be provided in the state budget of the Republic of Croatia without adequate funding of the defence system, the military obligation system and then parts of the Armed Forces subject to the equipment, training and preparedness programmes that Mr Bozinović spoke of on behalf of the HDZ Club will be significantly violated.
